Executive Summary
        The Committee on Oversight and Government Reform (Committee), led by Chairman
James Comer, has investigated (i) the alleged mismanagement of the federal government’s
investigation into Jeffrey Epstein and Ghislaine Maxwell, (ii) the circumstances and subsequent
investigations of Epstein’s death, (iii) the operation of sex trafficking rings and ways for the
federal government to effectively combat them and to reform the use of non-prosecution
agreements and/or plea agreements in sex-crime investigations, (iv) ways in which Epstein and
Maxwell sought to curry favor and exercise influence to protect their illegal activities, and (v)
potential violations of ethics rules related to elected officials. The Committee has conducted
transcribed interviews and depositions, reviewed the public release of hundreds of thousands of
documents, and issued subpoenas to federal agencies, financial institutions, the Estate of Jeffrey
Epstein (Estate), and individual witnesses.

       The Committee’s investigation was prompted by the decades-long failures of individuals
and institutions charged with protecting vulnerable survivors and holding powerful offenders
accountable. At nearly every stage, the cases reviewed by the Committee raise troubling
questions about how Jeffrey Epstein and Ghislaine Maxwell, who sexually abused and trafficked
young women and girls, avoided accountability for so long.

                    Exposing the Tactics of a Prolific Sex Trafficking Operation

        Over the course of several decades, Epstein used various methods to recruit and entrap
young women into his web of abuse. Epstein strategized to target young women and girls who
lacked sufficient support, making them vulnerable to offers of financial stability, housing, and
affection. Epstein proceeded to dangle promises of career opportunities and comfort until the
survivors of his crimes became completely dependent and subservient to his wishes. One of these
survivors, Sarah Kellen, testified to not realizing she was under his control until it was too late. 1

        Epstein would also recruit by presenting himself as a Victoria’s Secret scout to young
women, garnering credibility from his connection to his then-client and Victoria’s Secret CEO,
Leslie Wexner. 2 According to Committee-obtained testimony, Epstein would request that young
women meet him in hotel rooms or in his home for an “interview,” where he would ask them to
undress, and in some cases physically assault them, under the guise of judging their bodies for
potential modeling opportunities. 3 Wexner claimed during testimony that he approached Epstein
about this, but Epstein denied the fraudulent activity, and this lie was enough to satisfy Wexner’s
inadequate investigation. 4

       Kellen, whose testimony before the Committee exposed this abuse, operated under
Epstein’s thumb for years and obeyed his orders for fear of the consequences of standing up to
such a powerful and well-connected man. Epstein’s extensive network of employees, social

connections, and financial resources enabled him to maintain constant and oppressive control
over the women he abused. Survivors describe psychological manipulation and complete control
over their bodies and finances. 5 Those assistants or employees whom he did not abuse played a
part, wittingly or unwittingly, in perpetuating the survivors’ dependency on Epstein by obeying
his orders to send cash, schedule hair and shopping appointments, set up travel with Epstein,
secure hotel rooms and apartments, and generally maintain their constant connection with him.

        Epstein was not the only abuser of his “personal assistants.” Kellen’s testimony before
the Committee revealed the names of two Epstein associates who she alleged sexually abused
her. As a result of Kellen’s testimony, the Committee referred both individuals to the Department
of Justice (DOJ) for further investigation.

        In addition to Epstein’s assistants who worked in a state of dependency, several of
Epstein’s other associates seemed to have simply blindly obeyed Epstein. The consequences of
this obedience were stark and devastating to not only his “personal assistants” but potentially
hundreds of women around the world. Maxwell and Epstein’s executive assistant, Lesley Groff,
was responsible for scheduling daily massages for Epstein with various women. 6 These
massages included those he specifically requested, those who called Groff and requested an
appointment to massage Epstein, and those whose contact information was stored under
“massage contacts” in what would be termed Epstein’s “Black Book”—a physical version of his
office directory created by Maxwell and maintained and updated by assistants like Groff. 7 The
names were categorized in the book by state or country, and certain identities contained
parentheticals signaling to Groff and other users to “be discreet.” 8 Despite these obvious warning
signs and Epstein’s conviction in 2008, Groff maintained to the Committee she had no
knowledge or suspicion during the course of her work for him that Epstein was capable of sex
crimes. 9 This testimony is not credible.

        Groff’s attitude is reflective of many others in Epstein’s orbit who, in the face of
numerous red flags, claimed that they never suspected Epstein was abusing women or
committing crimes of any kind. Two of Epstein’s closest employees, Richard Kahn and Darren
Indyke, testified they never witnessed any abuse by Epstein towards any young women or girls,
nor did they have reason to believe that his constant support and contact with so many young
women—even in light of his 2008 conviction—warranted any concern. 10 Prominent individuals
such as former President Bill Clinton, Leslie Wexner, Leon Black, Woody Allen, and Ariane de
Rothschild have all stated publicly or testified to the Committee they too had no knowledge of

              Conflicts of Interest in a Sweetheart Deal Shield and Enable Epstein

         State and local law enforcement identified substantial allegations of wrongdoing against
Epstein in the early-2000s in south Florida. Federal prosecutors at the United States Attorney’s
Office for the Southern District of Florida (USAO) and the Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI)
developed a parallel federal case against Epstein along with the ongoing state investigation. 12
However, former U.S. Attorney Alexander Acosta’s hands-off approach to the investigation and
his failures to oversee the daily involvement of those at the USAO left the investigation in the
hands of potential Epstein loyalists. The Committee uncovered evidence to suggest that Acosta’s
former assistant attorney and one of the senior decision makers in the Epstein case, Matthew
Menchel, had a connection to Epstein while he was at the USAO. 13 Other evidence indicates that
Bruce Reinhart, former attorney at the USAO during the time of the Epstein investigation, left
the USAO only to immediately be hired by Epstein to represent some of his associates in civil
cases. 14 That same evidence revealed that Reinhart was preparing to do this while still employed
at USAO, but Acosta was unable to confirm to the Committee whether Reinhart ever improperly
influenced the investigation because of Acosta’s own lack of involvement and knowledge of
Reinhart’s professional plans.

         Acosta, Menchel, and others at the USAO ultimately chose to end the investigation with
an unusually favorable non-prosecution agreement (NPA) that allowed Epstein to avoid federal
charges, extended federal protection in the Southern District of Florida to an unknown number of
“potential co-conspirators,” and left survivors uninformed while the agreement was negotiated. 15
In fact, Epstein included some of his assistants in the NPA without their knowledge or consent,
with them only learning of their inclusion many years later. 16 The use of the NPA to shield
Epstein and his associates by Acosta and the USAO undoubtedly allowed Epstein’s heinous acts
to continue for years without recourse.

        The consequences of the decision by the USAO and the FBI to use an NPA were
compounded by the failures of the State Attorney’s Office and the Palm Beach County Sherriff’s
Office in ensuring Epstein’s continuous and complete confinement for the 18 months of
incarceration agreed to in the NPA. Epstein ultimately served only 13 months of his 18-month

sentence in the Palm Beach County Stockade, during which he was permitted to spend
substantial amounts of time out on work release––for a sham corporation––both in an office he
established in Florida and his Palm Beach residence. 17 Acosta testified that his office received
multiple assurances that Epstein would not receive work release at all and expressed extreme
regret over his decision to entrust Epstein’s imprisonment to the state who proved to be an
“unreliable partner.” 18 Acosta stated that had federal prosecutors known the outcomes of their
state-based plea deal, they would never have resolved the case in the manner they did. 19

        Epstein’s excessive work release reflected preferential treatment of a wealthy man who
was allowed to continue his abuse of women even while incarcerated. 20 Kellen and several other
survivors came forward about the abuse they suffered at the hands of Epstein while he was on
work release in his South Florida office, his Palm Beach residence, or even, in Kellen’s case,
over video from the stockade itself. 21 Clearly, Epstein’s influence was not diminished by his time
in prison but instead seemed to flourish even after his release in 2009.

                         Friends in High Places and Institutional Corruption

         The Committee’s investigation uncovered how Epstein used wealth, intimate
connections, and social and professional prominence to conceal his crimes. Epstein continuously
built, developed, and exploited a network of wealthy and connected individuals. In doing so, he
prioritized power, fame, and wealth, even limiting his clients to those with net worths of at least
$1 billion. 22 Wexner serves as a central example of the connections Epstein established to
cement his network’s prominence and gain intimate access to their lives: in Wexner’s case, it was
turning over to Epstein control of Wexner’s family foundation and full power of attorney over his
finances. 23 Other witnesses before the Committee testified how Epstein cultivated dependencies
throughout his elite network by offering bespoke services, generous gifts, and sometimes access
to young women and girls. 24

       Significantly, Epstein did not operate alone. He relied on others in his ploy to recruit and
abuse young models, most notably, with convicted sex trafficker Jean-Luc Brunel and his

        The Committee found that Epstein used certain code names, largely based on fictional
princess names, to communicate with his associates about women he employed or knew. 27 The
content of these communications suggested that Epstein and others were discussing their sexual
relations with these women, and Epstein appeared to be offering them more women. One of these
associates was Jes Staley, a liaison at JPMorgan Chase who frequently communicated with
Epstein on behalf of the bank, who incredibly claimed to know absolutely nothing about emails
he exchanged with Epstein in which they appeared to be discussing sexual relations with one of
Epstein’s assistants dressed as a Disney princess character. 28

        Some Epstein associates questioned by the Committee seemingly claimed in their
testimonies that a man with connections to global political leaders, major philanthropists, titans
of industry, billionaires, Hollywood celebrities, and even a British prince could not be a sex
trafficker. 29 However, despite the repeated assertions of ignorance to Epstein’s crimes, these
same associates offered inadequate explanations for their continued close association with
Epstein after his 2008 conviction for soliciting prostitution from a minor.

        Following his conviction, Epstein seemingly upheld a professional, elite reputation while
continuing to display the behaviors, financial secrecy, and pursuits of young women and girls
commonly associated with a human trafficker. The Committee’s investigation found that while
some Epstein associates backed away from him, either gradually or suddenly after his 2008
conviction, others seemed unconcerned. For those who continued business as usual with
Epstein—such as Indyke, Kahn, Staley, Leon Black, or Kathyrn Ruemmler—Epstein ensured
they did not go unrewarded. Epstein sought complete loyalty and obedience from his staff and

friends by offering loans, personal favors, luxurious gifts, and white-glove services. By
accepting, these individuals not only turned a blind eye to his behaviors but assisted in
maintaining and concealing Epstein’s criminal life and enterprise.

        Upholding a network of prominence to cover a vast array of criminal sexual activity took
more than a few individuals to achieve. It also required financial institutions like JPMorgan
Chase and Deutsche Bank, which kept Epstein as a client after his 2008 conviction, to choose
profit and client introductions over clear red flags. 30 While some institutions cut ties prior to
Epstein’s final arrest in 2019, Epstein used his previous relationships to garner new financial
partners. Epstein’s intricate financial arrangements, international travels and visas, cash
movements, and political and institutional relationships served as tools that enabled his
avoidance of real legal accountability.

                                         Epstein’s Death in Prison

         The circumstances surrounding Jeffrey Epstein’s death revealed serious and longstanding
institutional deficiencies at the Metropolitan Correctional Center (MCC) and within the Federal
Bureau of Prisons (BOP). Evidence obtained by the Committee indicates that the failures of
August 9 and 10, 2019, leading to Epstein’s death while in custody, resulted from a series of
institutional failures at the facility. 31 Severe staffing shortages, overworked correctional officers,
inadequate training, deficient recordkeeping practices, and security procedures that were
inconsistently and improperly performed led to a dysfunctional system rife with error. 32

        In her testimony to the Committee, former MCC Correctional Officer and the last person
to perform a check on Epstein’s cell the night he died, Tova Noel, described this dysfunction as
“the MCC way”—an institutional culture in which departures from established policies and
procedures had become routine. 33 She described an institution responsible for creating conditions
in which serious lapses were not only possible, but increasingly normalized, leading to Epstein’s
death.

        On the circumstances and causes of Epstein’s death, the Committee was unable to
definitively dispel certain theories from the testimony it received. 34 Neither Noel nor former
Attorney General Bill Barr could identify the orange figure captured on video surveillance
equipment moving in the area near Epstein’s cell the night before he died. Testimony could also
not account for the lack of video surveillance footage from the malfunctioning or inoperable
security cameras where Epstein’s cell was located, even though MCC personnel were aware of
their malfunction at the time and failed to fix them before Epstein’s death. 35 Evidence received
by the Committee did not disprove the finding of the joint investigation between the Southern

                                Transparency for the American People

        The Committee reviewed and released tens of thousands of never-before-seen files
obtained under subpoena from the Estate and the DOJ, conducted and subsequently published the
videos and transcripts of 19 depositions and transcribed interviews, and regularly met with
Epstein survivors throughout the investigation to ensure their perspectives and experiences were
considered. The Committee sought and obtained testimony from those with documented
connections to Epstein, Maxwell, and their crimes. That testimony revealed the extent of
Epstein’s network and his efforts to curry favor with prominent individuals to shield himself and
his criminal conduct from scrutiny.

        On November 19, 2025, President Trump signed into law the Epstein Files Transparency
Act (EFTA) which mandated the release of additional millions of pages of documents going back
decades to the public pertaining to Epstein and Maxwell. 37 The Trump Administration provided
public access to investigative records concerning Jeffrey Epstein and Ghislaine Maxwell,
advancing transparency into their criminal activities and the individuals and institutions
associated with them.

       Despite extraordinary efforts undertaken by DOJ staff to expedite the release of EFTA
records, questions remain as to whether the DOJ has released all relevant documents. Moreover,
given the speed with which EFTA records were released, several instances of deficient redaction
exposed sensitive information of survivors.

                                                      ***

        The investigation’s findings make clear that Epstein and Maxwell were largely able to
escape justice and accountability for so long due to their powerful connections and institutional
failures. The survivors of sex trafficking conspiracies deserve nothing less than full justice, and
that requires a government that acts appropriately and diligently within the confines of the law to
hold perpetrators accountable. In this report, the Committee makes various recommendations
resulting from its investigation, including strengthening protections for survivors of sex
trafficking, holding institutions accountable for detecting and preventing sex trafficking,
enhancing records preservation practices and financial transparency, and improving interagency
coordination in complex federal investigations.

       Given the extraordinarily extensive scope of Epstein’s network and the breadth of his
connections, there remain numerous individuals with potentially relevant information whom the
Committee has not yet had the opportunity to interview.

                                                                        12
Background
       The events giving rise to the investigations into and subsequent incarcerations of Jeffrey
Epstein and Ghislaine Maxwell have spanned over two decades. These events, including the
death of Epstein in 2019, ultimately gave rise to the Committee’s investigation into the federal
government’s handling of the investigations of Epstein and Maxwell. The following is a
chronology of major events and steps in the Committee’s investigation:

      March 2005: Law enforcement authorities in Palm Beach, Florida opened an
       investigation to address allegations that Jeffrey Epstein had sexually abused multiple
       underage girls. 38

      May 2006: State Attorney Barry Krischer sent the case to a grand jury. 39

      July 2006: Epstein was arrested after a grand jury indicted him on a single count of
       soliciting prostitution of a minor. The FBI opened an investigation after Krischer was
       accused publicly of giving Epstein special treatment. 40

      2007: Federal prosecutors worked to prepare an indictment against Epstein. However,
       rather than pursue a federal indictment, the United States Attorney’s Office entered into a
       non-prosecution agreement with Epstein. Under the agreement, Epstein avoided federal
       prosecution in exchange for pleading guilty to state charges in Florida. 41

      June 2008: Epstein pleaded guilty to two state charges: one count of soliciting
       prostitution and one count of soliciting prostitution of a minor. Epstein served his
       sentence in a work-release program where he was permitted to work from his Palm Beach
       office and return to the jail each night. 42

      July 22, 2009: Epstein was released from jail early and avoided further scrutiny for 10
       more years before he was ultimately arrested in July 2019. 43

                                                        ***

      July 6, 2019: Jeffrey Epstein was arrested and taken into custody by FBI agents and the
       NYPD’s Crimes Against Children Task Force at Teterboro Airport in New Jersey. Epstein

      July 18, 2019: A federal judge denied bail, citing Epstein as a serious flight risk and a
       danger to the community. 45

      August 10, 2019: In the early morning hours, Epstein was found deceased in his cell in
       the Special Housing Unit (SHU) at the New York MCC. 46

      July 2, 2020: Ghislaine Maxwell was charged with “enticing a minor to travel to engage
       in illegal sex acts,” “conspiracy to entice a minor to travel to engage in illegal sex acts,”
       “transporting a minor with the intent to engage in criminal sexual activity,” “conspiracy
       to transport a minor with the intent to engage in criminal sexual activity,” and “two
       counts of perjury” by federal prosecutors in New York. 47

      December 29, 2021: Maxwell was convicted by a jury of multiple charges, including sex
       trafficking, conspiracy, and transportation of a minor for illegal sexual activity. 48

      June 28, 2022: Maxwell was sentenced to 20 years in federal prison. 49

                                                      18
I.      Epstein and Maxwell’s Network Facilitated the Recruitment,
        Control, and Abuse of Young Women.

        The Committee examined the network of relationships, recruitment practices, and
mechanisms of control that enabled Epstein to maintain access to and abuse women in his orbit.
Epstein repeatedly abused many of the women he recruited as personal assistants by fostering
their dependency and exploiting their positions to keep them under his influence. Epstein
maintained extensive control over the lives of these young women, including their housing,
employment, finances, career opportunities, and physical appearance.

       Many of Epstein’s other loyal staff members, including Lesley Groff, Ghislaine Maxwell,
and other assistants, played a part in enabling Epstein. Though Groff represented to the
Committee that she lacked any knowledge of Epstein’s criminal conduct at the time, 86 the
evidence reviewed by the Committee suggests that both Maxwell and Groff played a role in
arranging Epstein’s daily massages. Other assistants were responsible for scheduling and
performing Epstein’s massages, although their participation was seemingly compelled rather than
voluntary.

        A. Epstein Disguised Sexual Abuse as Massages.
        In the early 2000s, Jeffrey Epstein received a massage every single day. 87 The Committee
understands Epstein to have kept a roster of young women and girls with whom he would direct
his staff to contact and schedule massages. 88 Epstein recruited these victims to provide him
“massages,” but such “massages…became increasingly sexual in nature and would typically
include one or more sex acts.” 89 Epstein sexually assaulted minors under the guise of massages
for years. 90 The DOJ found that, from 2002 to 2005, Epstein “sexually exploited and abused
dozens of underage girls by enticing them to engage in sexual acts with him in exchange for
money.” 91

        The investigation and arrest of Epstein in 2006 confirmed that Epstein recruited a vast
number of young women and girls from the local Palm Beach, Florida area and sexually
assaulted them under the pretense of a legitimate massage session. 92 The youngest victim to
come forward was just fourteen years old, with many victims recruited from local high schools. 93

Epstein paid each girl hundreds of dollars in cash per massage. 94 In 2008, Epstein pleaded guilty
to Florida state charges for one count of solicitation of prostitution with a minor and one count of
solicitation of prostitution. 95

        The individuals within Epstein’s orbit enabled his criminal activities with these young
victims. Groff, Epstein’s executive assistant for nearly two decades, was responsible for
contacting and scheduling the masseuses. 96 Hundreds of survivors have come forward in the
years since, sharing their stories about the sexual abuse they endured at the hands of Epstein
during these “massages.” 97

         Groff worked as Epstein’s executive assistant from 2001 until his death in 2019. 98 In a
transcribed interview, Groff recounted that, upon starting her job, it immediately became
apparent to her that “a massage would be part of his day, and it was all treated very normally.” 99
Groff further explained this was known by everyone in Epstein’s office and recalled how
“everybody took a hand in helping [her]” learn how to schedule massages for Epstein. 100 Groff
testified that scheduling massages was similar to any other appointment:

        Lesley Groff (June 9, 2026)

        Q.       Did you find anything about the massage scheduling to be odd
                 when you first started?

        A.       Absolutely not. It was just like another appointment. It was like
                 going to the gym. It was -- it was very normal. 101


       Groff and other assistants regularly scheduled massage appointments for Epstein at his
various residences. Epstein had residences in Manhattan, New York; Palm Beach, Florida; Paris,
France; New Mexico; and a private island in the U.S. Virgin Islands called Little St. James. 102
Groff was primarily responsible for scheduling the Manhattan townhome massage appointments
for Epstein but testified that it is possible she would have also scheduled massages at other
residences. 103

       Though Groff scheduled daily massages for Epstein for years, she testified that she never
asked for the ages of any of the masseuses when scheduling appointments, claiming she simply

asked if they were available for the appointment or not. 104 Furthermore, Groff informed the

         When the Committee asked Groff how she would schedule massages each day, she
responded, “[Epstein] would call [her]. And, when [Epstein] gave [her] his 20 pages worth of
notes to schedule for the day, that would just be one of the things to schedule.” 107 Groff informed
the Committee that Epstein would typically provide her with the name and phone number of the
individual from whom he wished to receive a massage. 108 Groff would contact these masseuses
herself, or, on occasion, they would reach out to her to schedule the massage appointments. 109 In
order to contact the “masseuses,” Groff accessed the electronic version of the office directory
that maintained an ongoing list of names and contact information for individuals in Epstein’s
orbit. 110

        In addition to the online version, there was a physical version of the directory. The
physical directory was printed out and bound together into what is frequently referred to as the
“Black Book.” 111 This Black Book was kept in various places, including in Epstein’s offices, on
Groff and other assistants’ desks, and around Epstein’s homes. 112 According to Groff’s
testimony, she believed Maxwell to be the original creator of the Black Book, while other
assistants—including Groff—had the ability to access and edit the information within. 113

       Multiple lists of masseuse contacts exist within the Black Book, each with at least twenty
names, in various locations. 114 The following is an excerpt from the Black Book, specific to
massage contacts in New York:

        Groff claims that she was not aware of Epstein’s ongoing scheme to recruit and abuse
young women and girls. 119 The massage appointments with young women and girls, including
minors, took place inside Epstein’s homes, specifically his Manhattan townhome and Palm
Beach residence. 120 Epstein maintained a massage room where the massages and subsequent
abuse took place at these locations, and at least one witness recalled interacting with Groff at
Epstein’s Manhattan townhome. 121 Because Groff worked as Epstein’s executive assistant for
nearly twenty years, 122 it is unlikely that Groff knew nothing about Epstein and Maxwell’s abuse
of young woman and girls, and more likely that she provided false or misleading information to
the Committee regarding her knowledge of Epstein’s criminal conduct.

         B. Epstein’s Relied on His Personal “Assistants” and Recruiters to
            Identify and Control His Victims.
        Epstein surrounded himself with numerous young female assistants who were expected to
accompany him, travel with him, and attend to a wide range of personal tasks for him. Among
others, a few individuals that worked as assistants to Epstein included Sarah Kellen,
                                                                and              . Epstein’s
recruitment model entailed careful cultivation of a network of young women whom he recruited
to work as his personal assistants. Over time, many of these women came forward recounting
experiences of manipulation, exploitation, and abuse at the hands of Epstein and Maxwell.

         Survivors of Epstein and Maxwell’s abuse have publicly described the degradation and
torment of being an assistant in Epstein and Maxwell’s orbit. One anonymous survivor recalls
Epstein describing his operation as a cult, calling himself the “cult leader.” 123 She stated,
“[Epstein] controlled their finances, dictated who they saw and psychologically demeaned
them.” 124 She continued, “He monitored their bodies obsessively…and forced her to have
unnecessary, disfiguring surgery.” 125 This account is consistent with testimony received by the
Committee.

        In a transcribed interview, Sarah Kellen informed the Committee that during her time as
one of Epstein’s assistants, she was subject to the whims of Epstein and Maxwell at all hours,
and this remained the case for the entirety of her employment. 126 She described the expectations
of her role as Epstein’s assistant and explained how she felt pressured by Epstein’s constant
demands. 127 Kellen felt that whenever Epstein summoned her, she had to act immediately to
avoid upsetting him: 128

traveling the globe, or attending social outings. Many witnesses that visited Epstein’s residences
or traveled with him recalled seeing these young women around Epstein and typically referred to
them as “traveling assistants.” 134 Those who worked closely with Epstein repeatedly witnessed
his unusual relationships with these young women and concerning occurrences that should have
raised serious questions about the nature of these relationships.

indicators of human trafficking, Epstein’s associates seemingly chose to feign ignorance.

opportunities for exploitation, assaulting and abusing them in much the same manner as Epstein
himself. Kellen testified about the abuse she endured by Philip Levine, former Mayor of Miami,

        Epstein’s treatment of his personal assistants revealed another pattern of exploitation: the
use of financial control and abuse to maintain power over them. Kellen testified she was
subjected as an assistant to unfair working conditions, including low pay and even periods where
she received no compensation. 139 Epstein’s extensive control over these young women created an
environment in which leaving became impossible. His assistants feared him, yet they also
became dependent on him for virtually every aspect of their lives––as he controlled their
resources, jobs, income, housing, and general livelihood. For decades, Epstein maintained
control over every aspect of the lives of these young women, intentionally and strategically
cutting them off from society to dominate them emotionally and physically—all while avoiding
scrutiny from associates and law enforcement for his heinous behaviors.

        C. Epstein’s Network of Abuse was Self-Perpetuating.
        The defining characteristic of Epstein’s abuse network was manufacturing total financial,
emotional, and professional dependency for the young women and girls he targeted. Through the
use of gifts, cash payments, promises of opportunity, manipulation tactics, and even acquiring
visas or other immigration documents, Epstein established relationships that strengthened his
control over survivors of his abuse. For years, the U.S. federal government and its investigative
agencies failed to act on clear signals that Epstein and Maxwell were trafficking young women
into the United States.

i. The Recruitment Process
         Public reporting describes how Epstein “groomed, recruited, and plucked [girls] from
trailer parks, broken homes and the streets to sell sex for money.” 140After his 2008 conviction,
Epstein continued to recruit young women and girls from vulnerable circumstances, whom he

        Not only was Epstein’s abuse of Kellen part of a larger pattern in which he preyed on
vulnerable young women by exploiting their emotional and financial needs, he also dominated
the financial and personal lives of these young women to the point of indentured servitude. For
years, Epstein avoided scrutiny for his actions while his survivors were left destitute without any
sense of justice or accountability. The recruitment Kellen described to the Committee was
consistent with a pattern documented across multiple survivors’ accounts in which Epstein
sought out women experiencing financial or personal hardships and exploited those
vulnerabilities to facilitate ongoing abuse.

ii. Epstein Posed as Victoria’s Secret Scout to Recruit Models.
        Epstein also recruited women into his orbit by posing as a Victoria’s Secret modeling
scout. By selling himself as a modeling scout, Epstein was able to meet with and take advantage
of numerous young women aspiring to be models.

        In a deposition taken by the Committee, Wexner testified that he met Epstein sometime
between 1980 and 1985 through an introduction by Bob Meister. 157 Epstein began serving as
Wexner’s “family office manager” around 1987 where he “oversaw investment, oversaw people
getting paid, made sure taxes were done, and talked to accountants” for years. 158 Wexner became
Epstein’s most prominent client, bolstering his reputation with other wealthy individuals.

        Epstein used his relationship with Wexner to further his access to young women, but he
also used it to build credibility. Wexner’s corporation, L Brands, owned Victoria’s Secret, a
premier women’s lingerie brand. 159 Epstein held himself out as an affiliate, talent scout, and
recruiter for Victoria’s Secret due to his management of Wexner’s assets. 160 He allegedly used
this fraudulent role to approach young women under the pretense of offering high-profile
modeling careers. 161 According to Wexner, Epstein never worked as a talent scout or recruiter for
Victoria’s Secret. 162

       Kellen testified to the Committee that Epstein’s documented relationship with Wexner
and L Brands leadership lent credibility to this recruiting scam:

         This is only one example where Epstein leveraged his relationships with his associates
and affiliated businesses to enhance his credibility, which he then translated into access to
sexually abuse young women. In addition to exploiting certain connections to individuals
associated with the hair and underwear modeling industry, Epstein also maintained direct
relationships with other influential individuals in the modeling and fashion industry who faced
allegations of misconduct. 176 For example, Jean-Luc Brunel was a French modeling agent and a
close associate of Epstein. 177 Brunel visited Epstein almost 70 different times while Epstein was
jailed in Palm Beach after 2008. 178 Meanwhile, Epstein helped coordinate models for Brunel, 179
provided Brunel with the financial backing he needed to develop MC2 Model Management, 180
and even gave Brunel an apartment at the East 66th Street location where other young models, 181
including victims of Epstein, were housed. 182

        As part of his recruitment scheme, Epstein connected young aspiring models to Brunel
with promises of career opportunities at MC2, while paying for and assisting them with
acquiring visas to work as models. 183 Groff facilitated this activity by providing Epstein with
photographs of young models, thereby helping connect him to potential targets for abuse, and by
arranging the apartments for “Mr. Brunel’s models” at the East 66th Street location. 184 Evidence
indicates that Epstein operated this scheme––including potential visa and immigration fraud––
with Brunel to recruit young foreign models into the United States, to facilitate his access to and
exploitation of these vulnerable individuals.

       The Committee reviewed extensive documentation demonstrating that Epstein was also
connected to Daniel Siad, another French modeling scout. For years, Siad maintained a similar
scheme to that of Epstein and Brunel, under which Siad continuously provided Epstein with
photographs of models and arranged meetings between Epstein and the models, including after

who he recruited into his orbit and subjected to abuse and exploitation.

         D. Epstein Exercised Various Elements of Control.
       Epstein systematically structured every aspect of a woman’s life if he wished to control
them. He arranged housing, facilitated professional opportunities, dictated their social networks,
and even their appearances. As previously described, 208 Epstein created an environment for these
vulnerable young women to enter his orbit and left them unable to leave it using various
elements of control.

i. Housing
         Epstein used housing as a means of exerting control over the young women or girls in his
life, including his assistants. He dictated where the women were required to live and typically
paid for their housing. 209 During its investigation, the Committee learned that Epstein’s brother,
Mark Epstein, owned an apartment building to which Epstein had access. 210 Epstein used those
apartments to house a wide range of individuals associated with him, including employees,
assistants, women with whom he was involved romantically, and others. This arrangement
provided Epstein with additional means of control over the young women he housed, who in turn
became even more dependent on Epstein.

        According to public reporting and documents reviewed by the Committee, Mark Epstein
owned most of the units in an apartment building located at 301 East 66th Street in Manhattan,
New York, through his development company, Ossa Properties. 211 While Ossa Properties owned
the units to which Epstein had access, “Epstein effectively control[ed] them.” 212 Kellen testified
that Epstein had around ten apartments at the 301 East 66th Street location and housed many
individuals, including herself, his pilots, his girlfriend, his private chef, and others. 213 However,
recently unsealed court documents show that Epstein also “kept some of his underage victims in
an apartment building just 10 blocks from his Upper East Side townhouse,” 214 referring to the
301 East 66th Street location.

        During the investigation, the Committee inquired about Kellen’s living situation during
her time working as an assistant to Epstein. She informed the Committee that Epstein was
“completely dominating of everything in [her] life” and she “had to live where [Epstein] told
[her] to live”: 215

                 he place the apartment in your name?

        A.       He never put it in my name, although he always dangled that carrot
                 in front of me. In the beginning of my employment, he said, “If you

                 And then he kind of continued in the later years, as well; he kept
                 saying he was going to put the apartment in my name. But he never

         As previously described, Epstein created an environment of coercion and false promises
to keep the women around him loyal. Kellen explained that Epstein made repeated promises that
if she reached a certain number of years as his employee, he would place the apartment in her
name. 217

       Epstein provided housing to the young women in his orbit as a means of controlling
them. By removing their ability to select and finance their own living situation, Epstein deprived
them of their independence. This reliance on Epstein for everything meant they were unable to
escape Epstein’s abuse or come forward about the crimes perpetrated against them and others
without risking homelessness.

ii. Career Opportunities
       Epstein used promises of a brighter future and career opportunities to recruit and control
young women. As described above, many of the young women Epstein recruited into his orbit
worked under his employ as personal assistants. In addition to the opportunity to work directly
for Epstein, Epstein also often provided these young and vulnerable women with additional
education by paying for their tuition and continuously made promises to connect them with
successful individuals or companies in the modeling world. 218

        Kellen testified that Epstein and Indyke created her own design company, SLK Designs,
during her employ for Epstein. 219 Kellen informed the Committee that Epstein required her to
hire another assistant of Epstein’s to work for her at the design company. 220 While this assistance
appeared on its face to be a beneficial career opportunity for Kellen, it was merely another means

        Many survivors have since come forward to reveal how Epstein “dangled admission to

were always attached to Epstein’s offer to pay tuition and if they did not comply with Epstein’s

sexually abusing them. 228

        Epstein’s offers of employment, promises of access to the modeling industry, and
payment of young women’s tuition only served as mechanisms of control, reinforcing their
dependence on him for financial support, employment opportunities, and professional success.
Epstein intentionally cultivated a sense of indebtedness in these young women, which he then
used to enable sexual abuse.

iii. Access to Influential People
        Epstein’s network was extensive. He cultivated relationships with influential individuals
across political, financial, academic, business, scientific, entertainment, and even royal circles.
His associations included politicians and government officials, like Ehud Barak, 229 Secretary
Hillary Clinton, 230 President Bill Clinton, 231 and Sultan Ahmed bin Sulayem; 232 billionaires and

meetings with powerful and influential people. With politicians, world leaders, academics, and
billionaires on his side, Epstein seemed truly invincible and inescapable to his targets.

        In addition to her salary, Kellen testified that Epstein provided her with various gifts
during the time that she worked as his personal assistant. 241 Kellen testified that before she
started working for Epstein, he paid off her credit card debt, approximately one thousand five
hundred dollars. 242 The other gifts included jewelry, clothes, luggage, and a Mini Moke utility

v. Physical Appearances
        In addition to providing young women and girls in his orbit with housing, gifts, and other
material benefits, Epstein also dictated their appearances as a mechanism of control. Epstein
dictated what the young women around him wore and even dictated how they styled their hair,
completely restricting their ability to make independent decisions about their own appearances.
During her testimony to the Committee, Kellen explained how Epstein used this tactic to deprive
his targets of autonomy:

        Sarah Kellen (May 21, 2026)

        Q.       [C]an you briefly describe the nature of your relationship with
                 Jeffrey Epstein?

        A.       He was terrifying. He was completely dominating of everything in
                 my life. I had an apartment to live in in New York. So I had to live
                 where he told me to live. He dictated what I wore. If I would wear
                 something that he didn’t approve of, he would look at me and say,
                 “It’s disgusting. Where did you get that? Throw it away.” So every
                 time I would buy anything new, I would have to immediately go to
                 him to get approval. He dictated my haircut, my hair color. He would
                 go to the salon with me and tell them what color to make it, what cut
                 to make it. He gave me a boy cut with bangs, blonde hair, brown
                 hair, and every length, every cut. 248

        E. Executive Assistants Operated Behind the Scenes to Facilitate
           Epstein’s Abuse.
        Epstein’s personal assistants were tasked with demanding workloads, and many have
since come forward as survivors of Epstein’s abuse. However, Epstein’s executive assistants,
Groff and Maxwell, were active participants who created and maintained the infrastructure that
enabled Epstein to recruit, exploit, and abuse young women and girls. Through their roles in
coordinating appointments, arranging travel, managing communications, and facilitating access
to Epstein and his properties, Groff and Maxwell helped sustain the logistical and administrative
systems through which Epstein perpetrated his abuse over many years.

        Groff worked as an executive assistant to Epstein for almost twenty years, but this role
maintained distinct responsibilities and expectations from those of Epstein’s other assistants. As
previously described, Groff was in charge of scheduling all of Epstein’s meetings, appointments,
and events, including his daily massages. 249 Groff also worked in a legitimate office capacity
alongside Epstein’s other formal employees, including his lawyers and accountants—specifically
Richard Kahn, Darren Indyke,                   , Emad Hanna, and Harry Beller—distinguishing her
role from the assistants who were expected to remain available to Epstein’s demands whenever
needed. 250 While Epstein’s regular assistants were required to travel with Epstein, with trips
occurring every two to three days, 251 Groff informed the Committee that she did not travel with
Epstein, as it was not part of her role. 252 It was on these travels that many of Epstein’s assistants
recount enduring sexual abuse by Epstein, or others. 253 Therefore, Groff maintained a distinct
role in supporting Epstein from the young women he recruited, groomed, and exploited as his
assistants––a role that enabled him to continue committing crimes while evading accountability.

         Unlike Groff, and other employees, who provided more limited administrative assistance,
Maxwell played an active and significant role in facilitating and enabling his abuse of young
women and girls. Prior to Groff’s employment, which began in 2001, Maxwell was primarily
responsible for recruiting young women and girls and scheduling them for massages with
Epstein. 254 As previously explained, Groff also testified that Maxwell was the person responsible
for creating the directories kept at Epstein’s properties and residences which maintained lists of
names and contact information for massages. 255 Therefore, Maxwell had a direct role in the
facilitation of the massage appointments that, in some instances, ultimately provided Epstein
with the opportunity to sexually abuse the young women and girls involved.

       Numerous Epstein survivors have also recounted that Maxwell was not merely Epstein’s
enabler and co-conspirator, but that Maxwell herself participated in, and in some cases, directly

facilitating Epstein’s demands in a harsh, intimidating, and demeaning manner.




                                                   44
II.     Accountability Gaps, Botched Investigations, NDAs, and
        Sweetheart Deals Left Epstein Survivors in the Dark.
        In 2005, law enforcement authorities in Palm Beach, Florida investigated allegations that
Jeffrey Epstein had sexually abused numerous underage girls. 261 The investigation ultimately
expanded beyond local law enforcement and involved the FBI and the United States Attorney’s
Office for the Southern District of Florida (USAO). 262 Rather than pursue a federal indictment,
the USAO entered into a non-prosecution agreement (NPA) with Epstein in 2007, publicly
termed the “sweetheart deal.” 263 Under the agreement, Epstein avoided federal prosecution in
exchange for pleading guilty to state charges in Florida. 264 In 2008, Epstein pleaded guilty in
state court and was sentenced to a term of incarceration of 18 months, during which he was
granted extensive work-release privileges. 265 Epstein was released after serving only 13 months
because he received Florida “gain time”—sentence credit for good behavior—under the ordinary
rules applicable to county inmates and from which he was not precluded under the NPA. 266

        Additionally, four women—Sarah Kellen,                       , Lesley Groff, and
                                                        267
     —were granted federal immunity under the NPA. The NPA identified these women as
“potential co-conspirators” that could not be prosecuted for any crimes relating to Epstein’s
charges. 268 However, the NPA was kept secret from the public, including the women named in
the agreement, for approximately a year. 269 Kellen testified to the Committee on the detrimental
effect this secret deal had on her:

        Sarah Kellen (May 21, 2026)

        Later that same year, the United States Government placed me into Jeffrey
        Eptsein's Federal non-prosecution agreement as a named coconspirator. I
        was not told this was happening. I was not asked about it. No one from law
        enforcement ever spoke with me, ever heard my side, ever asked me a single
        question. I did not even know my name was in that agreement until after it
        had been signed and released to the public.

        The Federal Government of the United States branded me a criminal in a
        secret deal with my own abuser, without ever once speaking to me. From
        the day Jeffrey Epstein found me until July of 2019, no one from the Federal
        Government, or any government for that matter, ever contacted me about

         anything, not once. I have spent every year since trying to live underneath

        The Committee investigated the circumstances surrounding the federal investigation, the
negotiation of the NPA, the lenient treatment of Epstein during his state sentence, and the impact
of these circumstances on survivors. In November 2020, the Department of Justice’s Office of
Professional Responsibility (OPR) released a report, titled Investigation into the U.S. Attorney’s
Office for the Southern District of Florida’s Resolution of Its 2006-2008 Federal Criminal
Investigation of Jeffrey Epstein and Its Interactions with Victims during the Investigation (OPR
Report), examining the USAO’s investigation of Epstein between 2005 and 2008. 271 OPR
identified significant deficiencies in the government’s handling of the case, including poor
judgment in the resolution of the federal investigation, but did not conclude that former U.S.
Attorney Acosta engaged in professional misconduct. 272

        Upon review, the Committee finds that the crimes committed by Epstein after 2005 could
have been prevented and justice served had it not been for the failures of investigators and
federal prosecutors in South Florida and Washington, D.C. While some of these failures involved
an exercise in poor judgement by the USAO, others appeared to be the result of Epstein’s
pervasive and wide-reaching influence. These failures limited the Government’s ability to
determine the full scope of Epstein’s conduct and contributed to an outcome that did not
adequately account for the interests of survivors while enabling further criminal activity on the
part of Epstein and Maxwell.

         A. Acosta’s Failure to Oversee the USAO’s Federal Prosecution of
            Epstein Left the Investigation in the Hands of Probable Epstein
            Loyalists.
        Acosta’s lack of oversight allowed Epstein’s influence over certain individuals and
aspects of the investigation to affect the ultimate resolution of the case in ways that Acosta did
not fully understand at the time. Nevertheless, Acosta admitted to the Committee that he
understood he had the ultimate responsibility over decisions made at the USAO regarding
Epstein. 273

        Due to Acosta’s delayed participation in the case and his lack of knowledge as to the
number of people involved in the investigation, there were no measures in place at the USAO to
ensure the Epstein case—a matter involving sexual abuse of underage girls by a well-connected
billionaire—was insulated from corruption and conflicts of interest or the strong appearances
thereof. The USAO’s usual practice at the time meant the U.S. Attorney did not become involved

         In light of these apparent and serious conflicts of interest, it is suspect that Menchel was

resolve a case and confirmed in his testimony that such agreements are not typical in cases like
Epstein’s that involve sexual or violent crimes. 291 This conflict of interest combined with the
unusually lenient and atypical treatment of Epstein compared to other similarly situated criminals
raises questions that Menchel’s connection to Epstein may have impacted his recommendations
and handling of the Epstein case, including his unusual recommendation to enter into an NPA.

       Acosta’s approach––just as his approach in any other case––to the Epstein investigation

       Acosta’s failure to adequately oversee the USAO’s federal prosecution of Epstein may
have left the investigation in the hands of individuals with serious conflicts of interest, to include

         B. The NPA Agreement was Agreed to Prematurely,
            Inappropriately, and with Significant Elements of the
            Investigation Unresolved.
        The OPR Report did not uncover evidence of illegal action on the part of Acosta in the
making of the NPA, nor did it find that the NPA’s terms were legally invalid. 303 However, the
Committee determines that the NPA was an inappropriate and untimely resolution of the matter,
given the outstanding questions remaining in the investigation and the extent to which Epstein’s
crimes had not yet been fully examined.

        The OPR Report on the circumstances leading to the NPA highlighted that while the
execution of such a deal was in “poor judgement” on the part of Acosta and the USAO, the case
against Epstein was weakened by legal issues and potential defenses to each charge that
ultimately led to that deal being struck. 304

         During his transcribed interview, Acosta repeatedly cited the perceived unreliability of
certain survivors, and their apparent reluctance to testify against Epstein, that left the federal case
vulnerable to failure. 305 He testified that there was consensus within the USAO that the
evidentiary risks made a negotiated resolution preferable to a federal trial which they believed
could carry a significant risk of failure:

       However, evidence reviewed by the Committee tends to show that the USAO proceeded
with the NPA prematurely, as significant elements of the investigation were unresolved.

i.       Missing Evidence
        According to the OPR Report, USAO was aware of missing computers, hard drives, and
security camera footage from Epstein’s Palm Beach property that likely contained critical
evidence of sexual exploitation or child pornography and that could corroborate survivors’
statements. 307 After conducting a raid on Epstein’s residence in Palm Beach, Florida on October
20, 2005, officers from the Palm Beach Police Department (PBPD) reported that certain
equipment had been “removed” from the property. 308 The OPR Report stated that attorneys at the
USAO attempted to locate this equipment, theorized it held valuable and important evidence, and
even located an unnamed individual they believed to possess the evidence. 309 Unfortunately,
upon intense pushback from Epstein’s defense attorneys, and more significantly, a rush to
implement the NPA, these efforts were inexplicably abandoned by USAO. 310

        During his transcribed interview, Acosta testified that he did not know the identity of the
unnamed individual suspected of possessing the equipment nor the outcomes of the USAO’s
search for this equipment:

         Media reporting has raised the possibility that Samuel Richard “Sandy” Berger—former

        Berger passed away in 2015 328 and questions about the search remain unanswered. This
information should have been discovered at the time by agencies such as the USAO or the FBI,
both of which possess full criminal investigative authority, and were aware of or should have
been aware of the likelihood that Epstein received insider information. Yet neither agency
appears to have conducted such an investigation before the NPA was established or at any
subsequent phase of the investigation. Significant evidence was therefore left unexamined before
the case was resolved through the NPA, undermining the theory that an NPA was justified due to
insufficient evidence to warrant federal prosecution.

ii.     Failure to Coordinate with Other Jurisdictions
        The USAO failed to coordinate with other jurisdictions that may have had evidence of
Epstein’s criminal activities that would have tended to favor a broader federal prosecution.
According to the OPR Report, the USAO knew of allegations that Epstein’s crimes extended
beyond Florida into other states including New Mexico and New York. 329 In his transcribed
interview, Acosta testified that he had no knowledge of out-of-state allegations:

        Alexander Acosta (September 19, 2025)

                 in 1997, Maria Farmer alleging that Epstein assaulted her in
                 New York as a minor and Annie Farmer alleging that Mr.
                 Epstein assaulted her in New Mexico as a minor. So my
                 understanding of those allegations…is that they were flown to
                 those locations, not with Epstein, but met Epstein there.

iii.     Failure to Conduct Interviews with Epstein Associates

with significant figures in Epstein’s orbit, prior to the execution of the NPA, and ignored
evidence tending to show that Epstein may have been intimidating or tampering with key
witnesses. The Committee conducted depositions and transcribed interviews with numerous
individuals within Epstein’s orbit. Among others, this included former President Bill Clinton, Les
Wexner, Darren Indyke, Richard Kahn, Ted Waitt, Sarah Kellen, Lesley Groff, Doug Band,
Kathryn Ruemmler, Bill Gates, Leon Black, and Jes Staley. 332 The Committee asked witnesses

         C. The Circumstances of Epstein’s Work Release in 2008
            Amounted to an Irresponsible Abuse of Discretion by the Palm
            Beach County Sheriff’s Office and a Serious Lack of Judgement
            by the USAO in Entrusting Epstein’s Imprisonment to the State
            System.
       The Palm Beach County Sheriff’s Office (PBSO) was technically within its authority to
grant Epstein’s application for work release based on policies in place at the time, 346 but the
decision was an abuse of its discretion considering facts known to the PBSO and the resulting
absurd leniency that enabled Epstein to commit further crimes.

        The strange circumstances of Epstein’s work release included the following terms:
Epstein could leave the stockade for up to 12 hours a day, six days a week to purportedly work at
the, by all accounts fictitious, Florida Science Foundation; 347 he traveled by private car and was
accompanied or monitored by off-duty sheriff’s deputies whom he paid and even gifted

        According to the OPR Report, the USAO initially treated Epstein’s work release as a
possible breach of the NPA serious enough to reopen the federal prosecution but ultimately
decided against this position. 365 In fact, on November 24, 2008, Acosta sent a formal notice to
Epstein attorney, Roy Black, stating that “the Office believes that Mr. Epstein’s application to
and participation in the work release program is a material breach of the Non-Prosecution
Agreement.” 366 However, this issue was never resolved, and the USAO did not produce any
formal documentation reflecting its ultimate decision to decline prosecution or setting forth the
basis for that determination. The shift away from pursuing prosecution occurred after Acosta
recused himself from Epstein-related matters in late November 2008 and First Assistant U.S.
Attorney Jeffrey Sloman became the U.S. Attorney and senior decisionmaker. 367

        At no point between 2009 and 2019 did any attorney at the DOJ or investigator at the FBI
ever seek to re-open Epstein’s case and prosecute a breach of his NPA. In fact, according to
every U.S. attorney general that served between 2009 and 2019, there was no involvement in any
investigation into Epstein until 2019 when Bill Barr was appointed U.S. Attorney General. 368
Even Barr only ever discussed the case at a broad level and was never deeply involved. 369

                                                        66
         D. A Lack of Information about the NPA and the Existence of Non-
            Disclosure Agreements (NDAs) Prevented Survivors from
            Receiving Justice and Delayed Exposure of Epstein’s Crimes.
        In 2019, the U.S. District Court for the Southern District of Florida held that federal
prosecutors at the USAO violated the Crime Victims’ Rights Act (CVRA) by entering the NPA
without first conferring with Epstein’s survivors. 370 Significantly, the court found the USAO
acted to “conceal the existence of the [NPA] and mislead the victims to believe that federal
prosecution was still a possibility.” 371 Keeping survivors in the dark about Epstein’s “sweetheart
deal” created opportunities for Epstein to continue his crimes and avoid civil liability and public
scrutiny for even longer.

        Acosta testified that he ultimately approved the decision by USAO attorneys to cease
notifying the survivors of Epstein about the NPA, because it contained a provision that allowed
the survivors to recover financially in the event of Epstein’s guilty plea. 372 USAO attorneys were
concerned that notifying the survivors of their potential financial recovery under the NPA could
allow the defense to challenge their credibility or suggest potential bias based on their financial
interest. 373 Ultimately, the USAO for the Southern District of Florida believed such challenges
could complicate Epstein’s then-pending appeal of the agreement before the DOJ in the District
of Columbia. 374

        The Committee maintained communication with survivors of Epstein’s crimes throughout
its investigation, and many expressed their frustrations that very few of them were ever
interviewed or even contacted by law enforcement agencies during the Epstein investigation
prior to 2019. 375 It also appears that survivors were further kept in the dark by the existence of
NDAs with certain individuals barring them from disclosing any prior sexual contact. The
Committee reviewed evidence that Epstein may have assisted these individuals in crafting and
implementing these NDAs.

       As an example, Black, former client and associate of Epstein, formed an NDA with a
Russian woman whom he had an extramarital sexual affair with over the course of six years. 376
The NDA went into effect in 2015 and its terms required that Black pay the woman $100,000 per
month for 15 years, $1 million in loan forgiveness, and $2 million in British pounds for a visa in
the United Kingdom in exchange for her silence on their past relationship. 377 At that time,

Government Reform, H.Res.1543, 119th Cong.2 (Sept. 16, 2026).

                                                     68
III. Epstein Used Connections as Currency and Enablers Kept the
     Money Flowing.
        According to documents and testimony received by the Committee, Epstein intentionally
made himself useful to powerful people, used those relationships to enhance his own credibility,
and spent that credibility to gain more access. The result was a self-reinforcing network that
helped sustain Epstein’s financial and institutional position long after his abuse of young women
and girls became a matter of public record.

        Epstein surrounded himself with wealthy and prominent associates. He built and
maintained those relationships deliberately and misrepresented the nature and extent of those
relationships to serve specific functions: they made his financial activities appear legitimate,
provided social cover, and made scrutiny appear implausible.

        Epstein cultivated an image of extraordinary influence. For decades, he presented himself
as an elite financial adviser, philanthropist, and confidant to some of the world’s wealthiest
individuals, political leaders, academics, business executives, and cultural figures. These
relationships provided Epstein with credibility, making him appear financially sophisticated,
socially accepted, and institutionally legitimate. Epstein cultivated an appearance of legitimacy
that obscured the reality of his conduct. Those relationships became a form of social and
financial capital that he repeatedly leveraged to attract clients, ensnare young women, discourage
scrutiny, and preserve access to the institutions necessary to sustain his operations. Epstein
treated connections as a form of currency. Proximity to one influential person helped him gain
access to another, and each new relationship enhanced the apparent legitimacy of the network.

        The significance of that network became even more pronounced after Epstein’s 2008
conviction for soliciting prostitution from a minor. His criminal conduct was no longer merely
the subject of accusation or rumor; it was a matter of public record. Yet associates continued to
conduct business with Epstein, accepted his referrals, sought his financial advice, socialized with
him, partnered with him in investments, or otherwise maintained relationships that helped restore
his standing within elite circles. In several instances, these relationships expanded after his
conviction.

        Epstein’s network was more than a collection of prominent names. It was infrastructure
that supported his criminal enterprise. Wealthy clients supplied revenue and validation.
Professional advisers administered his entities and transactions. Banks moved his money.
Academic institutions offered prestige and access. Employees maintained his residences,
schedule, and travel. Prominent associates helped preserve the appearance that Epstein remained
accepted within elite society even after his conviction.

       A. Epstein Deliberately Cultivated Elite Relationships and Used
       Them to Establish Financial and Social Legitimacy.
      Epstein engaged in four patterns to enable his criminal activities: (1) Epstein structured
many of his closest relationships so that the other party was dependent on him for money,

                                                 69
financial advice, professional opportunities or advancements, introductions, investments,
charitable contributions, or personal favors; (2) powerful associates maintained contact with
Epstein after his 2008 conviction for soliciting prostitution from a minor, permitting him to
revive his operations; (3) Epstein indebted a fiercely loyal staff to keep his enterprise running;
and (4) Epstein’s prominence was not separated from his trafficking, but in fact actively enabled
it by providing social cover, reducing the likelihood of challenge, and offering targets of his
abuse and observers false reassurance about his conduct. The strength of Epstein’s network of
influential connections made survivors of his crimes reluctant to come forward.

i.      Epstein Intentionally Cultivated and Structured Dependency.
       Epstein structured many of his closest relationships so that the other party was dependent
on him for money, introductions, or for professional advancement. This accumulation of contacts
began early in Epstein’s career as an alleged “money manager,” 382 or a financial consultant for
wealthy individuals. 383 Epstein positioned himself as the person who could make an
introduction, structure a deal, solve a tax problem, connect one billionaire to another, or open a
door that would otherwise remain closed.

        The more powerful the people around him became, the more credible Epstein appeared.
No relationship did more to launch that image than Epstein’s relationship with his most
consequential client, Les Wexner, who gave Epstein extraordinary authority to manage his
financial affairs.

        In his deposition with the Committee, Wexner disclosed how he granted Epstein power of
attorney in 1991 384 and appointed him trustee of the Wexner foundation. 385 This authorized
Epstein to sign checks, hire staff, borrow money, and buy or sell property on Wexner’s behalf. In
a deposition with the Committee, Wexner confirmed that Epstein had “wide latitude” over his
financial affairs, extending to his children’s trust accounts. 386 Wexner also testified that Epstein
used this authority to steal millions from him:

        Leslie Wexner (February 18, 2026)

        Q.       Mr. Epstein was convicted in 2008. Did you maintain any
                 contact with Mr. Epstein after his plea deal?

        A.       As I recall, concomitantly with Abigail taking over the finances, my
                 wife is an M&A lawyer. She worked for Davis Polk. She’s really
                 smart. She took over the finances and kind of concomitantly she
                 was discovering that he stole money from us and concomitantly
                 finding out about these accusations and the criminal activity that he

Foundation funds without Wexner’s knowledge. 390 Although Wexner characterized Epstein’s
designation as trustee as a matter of simple administrative “convenience for signing
documents,” 391 the actual authority conveyed was substantial. Epstein acquired the ability to act
on behalf of one of the country’s wealthiest businessmen and leveraged his association with
Wexner as a credential in his dealings with others.

        The trust Wexner placed in Epstein elevated Epstein’s standing and allowed Epstein to
expand his self-reinforcing network. Unlike traditional wealth management firms, Epstein kept
an extraordinarily narrow, highly exclusive client roster consisting primarily of multi-
billionaires.

        Black was not alone among Epstein’s wealthy and well-connected associates in
continuing to engage socially and financially with him after his criminal conviction. The
Committee’s investigation also identified extensive financial and personal ties between Epstein
and individuals associated with the Edmond de Rothschild Group. According to documents
released pursuant to the EFTA, this included Fabrice Aidan, a former French diplomat and
employee at Edmond de Rothschild Group. 406 Kahn and Indyke also testified to the Committee

financial services. 407 Kahn testified that Ariane de Rothschild paid Epstein “20- or $25

Paris offices of Edmond de Rothschild in connection with an ongoing corruption and influence-
peddling investigation involving Aidan. 409 Aidan maintained extensive communications with
Epstein, including after his criminal conviction, and may have leveraged diplomatic access and
institutional affiliations in connection with those interactions. 410

financial sector. By maintaining relationships with influential technology executives alongside
financiers, industrialists, philanthropists, and world leaders, Epstein expanded the range of

further reinforced the perception that sophisticated business leaders continued to trust his

actuality to boost his social prominence, according to individuals interviewed by the
Committee. 414 For example, Bill Gates testified in his transcribed interview that “While

never reciprocated.” 415

         Nevertheless, these associations did strengthen Epstein’s reputation among prospective
clients, financial institutions, and professional advisers while reinforcing the perception that he
operated within the highest levels of international finance. Whether discussing existing clients or
seeking new ones, Epstein repeatedly relied upon the prestige associated with these relationships
to validate his own credibility and expand his influence.

        This exclusive client list provided the institutional and social cover that seemingly
blinded observers––and employees closest to him––to his crimes. As these relationships
accumulated, they became mutually reinforcing: the willingness of one billionaire to entrust
Epstein with sensitive financial matters encouraged others to do the same, while the continued
association of prominent clients provided legitimacy that obscured the reality of his trafficking
enterprise, even after Epstein’s criminal conduct became publicly known.

ii.     Certain Post-2008 Conviction Contacts with Epstein Escalated.
        In 2008, Epstein pleaded guilty to state charges in Florida related to soliciting prostitution
from an underage girl. 416 Epstein served only thirteen months in a county jail with extensive
work-release privileges and was subsequently required to register as a sex offender. 417 His status
as a convicted sex offender was a matter of public record from that point forward, which began

        Epstein repeatedly sought opportunities to engage with Lutnick and form a relationship,
given his prominence and wealth. However, Lutnick never fully engaged with him to the same
extent as others did. This behavior is consistent with a broader pattern the Committee observed
in Epstein’s interactions with and attempts to curry favor with other influential individuals.

         Bill Gates was first introduced to Epstein in 2011 through “people [Gates] trusted in [his]
professional and philanthropic work.” 459 In his transcribed interview, Gates testified that upon
this introduction, Gates knew Epstein had a criminal conviction of a sexual nature and a bad
reputation stemming from that conviction, 460 but still sought to establish a professional
relationship to “translate into meaningful philanthropic support.” 461

        According to emails produced by the DOJ pursuant to the EFTA, Epstein appeared to be
working with Boris Nikolic—a long-time advisor to Gates—and advising him on how to
negotiate his professional separation from Gates in 2013. 462 During these negotiations, Epstein
wrote draft emails seemingly in preparation to leverage sensitive information—including past
relationships—against Gates. 463 In a transcribed interview, Gates testified that he had no
recollection of seeing these emails prior to their release under the EFTA. 464 Gates stated that he
was never explicitly threatened, but it appeared from the emails as though Epstein contemplated
blackmailing him:

iii.     Epstein Built an Indebted and Fiercely Loyal Staff to Keep the Enterprise
         Running, Even After His Conviction.
        The engagement of high-profile associates rehabilitated Epstein’s post-conviction image
and revived his operations. This resurgence was only made possible by an inner circle of staff
that demonstrated a rare level of loyalty and who maintained their dedication for years after
Epstein’s 2008 guilty plea, including after his conduct became a matter of public record. These
staff members managed the daily mechanics, tax structures, cash disbursements, and scheduling
that kept his enterprise running.

        The Committee asked these employees why they remained in Epstein’s employ for so
long, given his legal issues, and, despite the fact that he was “extremely, extremely
demanding” 475 and “would call every 15 minutes” 476 to “give [] directives.” 477 Their responses
reveal unfettered personal favors, exuberant gifts, and a life of glamour that his employees could
not relinquish in the face of Epstein’s criminal prosecution.

        On March 19, 2026, the Committee deposed Epstein’s longest tenured professional
employee, Indyke, Epstein’s personal attorney. 478 During his deposition, Indyke testified that he
met Epstein in 1996 through work at his firm, Gold & Wachtel. 479 Indyke performed extensive
transactional and corporate work for Epstein, including investment agreements, loans, stock-
purchase agreements, options, and rights of first refusal. 480 Indyke became central to the legal
and financial architecture surrounding Epstein’s affairs. 481

        Furthermore, Indyke testified that his total compensation while in Epstein’s employ was
close to $30 million over the course of 23 years. 482 However, other causes for his loyalty
emerged during examination that extend well beyond conventional attorney-client compensation.
Indyke testified that Epstein was a “mentor” to him and provided “emotional and financial
support,” even paying for every infertility treatment until Indyke’s family was able to
conceive: 483

reputation by asking a bank exactly how to structure withdrawals to avoid reporting. 487

         Deutsche Bank employees flagged concerns, on at least one occasion, that Indyke visited
their bank to cash two checks in the amount of $7,500 and another in $4,000. 488 However,
Indyke explicitly mentioned that he would not cash the second check to avoid “paperwork” and
exceeding the “cashing limit” and then stated that he would cash the $4,000 check another
day. 489 The federal crime of cash structuring involves deliberately splitting cash transactions to
stay below Currency Transaction Report (CTR) $10,000 reporting threshold. 490 The FBI found
that as of October 22, 2019, Indyke had engaged in weekly $7,500 cash withdrawals on behalf of
Mr. Epstein. 491 During the Committee’s examination, Indyke acknowledged that he was cashing
checks below this threshold, and though he denied attempting to avoid the reporting threshold, he
never furnished a justification for cashing checks in this manner. 492

blind eye to the continuation of Epstein’s criminal enterprise due to these luxuries. Groff stated
to the Committee that the lifestyle and these gifts were offered to “control” her. 509

        Secondary to Groff, Kellen also facilitated scheduling for Epstein, 510 acted as one of
Epstein’s traveling assistants, 511 and wielded immense control over the daily movement of
survivors and visitors inside Epstein’s Manhattan and Palm Beach properties. In her June 2026
interview, Kellen testified regarding how she helped manage Epstein’s schedule, including
calling clients and setting appointments. 512

        Epstein employed his usual schemes of control over Kellen. However, the Committee’s
investigation uncovered a more perverse method of control in Kellen’s case compared to Groff.
During a transcribed interview, Kellen disclosed how she was groomed, psychologically
manipulated, and sexually abused by Epstein, describing how she was instructed to enforce strict
access barriers around Epstein’s inner circle while organizing the pipeline of women coming into
his environment. 513 According to documents and testimony, Epstein further cemented Kellen’s
dependency on him, assisting in the setup of her design firm, SLK Designs, controlling her
housing, and dictating her appearance. 514

         Epstein’s continued association with respected figures had operational consequences. It
caused employees and others to disregard blatant warning signs, preserved Epstein’s access to
institutions, and reinforced the appearance that he remained someone with whom prominent
people could safely associate. These employees were seemingly manipulated by various tools at
