Epstein’s disposal. These individuals, bound by financial incentives, personal favors, and

loyalty and complicity of his inner circle.

iv.      Epstein Used his Social Prominence as Operational Cover for Trafficking.
       Epstein’s social and professional prominence was not incidental to his trafficking
operation. It was structurally useful for it. Prominence provided at least three functional benefits:

      1. It reduced the likelihood that victims, staff, or observers would challenge his conduct;

      2. It gave him cover for travel and access to venues and institutions that would otherwise
         scrutinize a visitor more closely; and

      3. It gave bystanders a false signal of safety, on the theory that a man with such extensive
         and reputable associations was unlikely to be capable of or would be too exposed to the
         risk of sexual abuse and trafficking.

        The dynamics that were created by Epstein’s use of his social prominence were
corroborated by witnesses who interacted with Epstein directly. In Epstein’s early years of
operation, Wexner stated that “…as I look back at it, I was conned by the world Olympic all-time
con artist” 515 and “he had, in hindsight, multiple personalities,” 516 calling him “a crook and a con
man,” “diabolical,” and “sinister” 517

       This double life was a recurring theme described by those in Epstein’s inner circle.
Kellen and Groff echoed the description of Epstein as a “Jekyll and Hyde” figure who used
charm and volatility to maintain control. 518 Kellen stated the following to the Committee:

         Sarah Kellen (May 21, 2026)

         He was like Jekyll and Hyde. He could be the most charming, like playful,
         what I considered generous at the time; now I know it was more about
         control. You didn’t want to get on his bad side. He could turn at the drop of
         a hat, and you would see the fire in his eyes, and it was horrifying. I would
         witness him bring even Ghislaine to tears. 519

Evidence available to the Committee does not indicate any legitimate activities offered by the

organizations appear to have served as both Epstein’s cover to commit sexual crimes while on
work release and as a mechanism to pay off survivors. Yet Richard Kahn, Epstein’s accountant,
testified that setting up an intricate network of shell companies, pass through entities, and bank
accounts was “common practice” and part of “the ABCs of financial planning for wealthy
individuals like Epstein.” 525

        to Victims, and to Legitimize His Business Activities.
        Epstein systematically engaged with major financial and academic institutions. By
attaching his name and capital to world-renowned banks and universities, he bought prestige,

       The Committee identified three recurring categories of financial activity that repeatedly
drew the attention of bank compliance personnel: (1) recurring cash withdrawals, (2) foreign
wire activity, and (3) substantial recurring payments made to alleged co-conspirators and
modeling recruiters.

      1. Recurring cash withdrawals

        JPMorgan’s internal compliance records from 2006 identified recurring cash withdrawals
from Epstein’s accounts ranging from $40,000 to $80,000 per month. 533 In a transcribed
interview, Epstein’s banking associate, Staley, testified that he asked Epstein about his cash
withdrawals because he knew “cash withdrawals were a red light for the bank and…for
regulators.” 534

       According to a Consent Order issued by the New York State Department of Financial
Services (Consent Order), Deutsche Bank was also seemingly aware of suspicious cash
withdrawals from Epstein accounts. 535 The Consent Order detailed a total of 97 withdrawals
occurring two to three times a month from 2013 to 2017 in the amount of $7,500 by an Epstein
attorney. 536 In total, this amounted to more than $800,000 in cash withdrawals from Epstein
accounts. 537 During this period, the Consent Order noted that Epstein’s attorney inquired with
Deutsche multiple times how cash could be withdrawn “without triggering an alert.” 538 In
response to these inquiries, Deutsche was concerned that the pattern of cash withdrawals
appeared as “structuring” to avoid triggering CTRs. 539

      2. Foreign wire activity

dollars to foreign accounts in Russia, Ukraine, and Eastern Europe that were flagged as
suspicious by multiple banks, including JPMorgan Chase. Notably, the majority of JPMorgan’s
flags were not raised until after Epstein’s second arrest in 2019––indicating a complete
breakdown in process and oversight. 543

      3. Substantial recurring payments made to alleged co-conspirators and modeling

payments for people, both men and women who worked for him, who he knew, who were
acquainted with him.” 544 Despite internal compliance warnings that these transactions bore
hallmarks of human trafficking and illicit financial activity, JPMorgan leadership repeatedly
ignored these signs of criminal behavior opting to maintain Epstein’s accounts. 545

        The sheer scale of Epstein’s banking activity within major institutions provided an
unprecedented degree of operational flexibility for his enterprise. Internal JPMorgan records
indicate that Epstein maintained a network of at least one hundred thirty-four separate bank
accounts at the institution, which included accounts for his various shell companies, trusts, and
personal associates. 546 While Epstein was serving his 2008 jail sentence, JPMorgan continued to
process wire transfers from these accounts to entities in Russia and Eastern Europe. 547 This

        Epstein also formed connections with professors at prominent universities, made
donations to these universities, encouraged others to donate on his behalf, and used his
connections to help friends or staff gain access to these universities—either for themselves or
their families—to curry favor.

         At Harvard University, Epstein donated approximately $9.2 million, including $6.5
million to establish the Program for Evolutionary Dynamics, directed by Professor Martin
Nowak. 548 Between 2010 and 2018, Epstein visited the program’s offices more than 40 times,
frequently accompanied by a young female assistant. 549 He was provided a private office, keys,
and building access credentials that allowed him and his assistant to enter the facilities at any
time. 550 Similarly, at MIT, Epstein donated $850,000 between 2002 and 2017 and maintained
relationships with Media Lab director Joi Ito and professor Seth Lloyd, who facilitated at least
nine campus visits following his conviction. 551 MIT's independent investigation established that
senior administrators knowingly approved certain donations from Epstein despite his status as a
convicted sex offender, while witnesses reported that Epstein sometimes attended campus
meetings accompanied by young female assistants. 552

        Epstein also cultivated a relationship with Bard College President Leon Botstein,
beginning with a $75,000 contribution to Bard High School Early College in 2011. 553 Their
relationship continued for years and included numerous meetings, campus visits, and
introductions to wealthy donors and prospective students. 554 Documents released by the
Department of Justice revealed that Botstein assisted young female musicians connected to
Epstein, while separate correspondence demonstrated Epstein's ability to secure educational
introductions and admissions assistance through Bard's leadership. 555

         C. Epstein’s Reach was Global, and He Used International
         Connections and Pathways to Traffic Victims.
      Epstein’s network reached foreign heads of state and government, and he used
immigration pathways and international travel infrastructure to move victims across borders.

        In November 2010, just months after Epstein completed his sentence for soliciting
prostitution from a minor, Andrew Mountbatten-Windsor, formerly Prince Andrew, stayed at
Epstein’s Manhattan townhouse. 571 During this visit, Epstein hosted a formal dinner party in
honor of the prince, attended by prominent media personalities, journalists, and public figures. 572
The significance of the relationship with former Prince Andrew extended to Epstein’s
professional standing. Staley confirmed for the Committee that Epstein actively brokered these
elite connections for the bank, stating that, yes, “Epstein facilitated meetings between individuals
at J.P. Morgan with Andrew Mountbatten-Windsor.” 573 This high-profile association served as
immediate rehabilitation and proved to international observers and potential victims that Epstein
remained in good standing with the world’s most elite figures.

        Andrew Mountbatten-Windsor was accused of sexual abuse by Virginia Giuffre, first
allegedly meeting her in March 2001 when she was seventeen years old. 574 According to court
filings and her memoir, Giuffre alleged that the abuse took place on three separate occasions:
once in London, once in New York, and once on Epstein’s private island. 575 Giuffre claimed that
she was a victim of Epstein and Maxwell’s and was recruited into their orbit at the mere age of
sixteen. 576 She endured years of physical and psychological abuse at the hands of Epstein and
Maxwell. 577 Epstein and Maxwell introduced Giuffre to former Prince Andrew as part of their
scheme to recruit young women and girls for sexual abuse. 578

      Ehud Barak, former Prime Minister of Israel, repeatedly stayed at Epstein’s Manhattan
townhouse between 2013 and 2017, logging dozens of visits. 579 Epstein used his connection with

                                                         105
IV.     Epstein’s Sudden Death Exposed Institutional Failures at the MCC.
       On July 6, 2019, Epstein was arrested and charged with sex trafficking of minors and
conspiracy to commit sex trafficking. 614 Following his arrest, Epstein was detained at the
Metropolitan Correctional Center (MCC) in Manhattan, New York. 615 Though his lawyers
proposed a $100 million bond and house arrest, a federal judge denied his release on July 18,
2019, citing Epstein as a serious flight risk and a danger to the community. 616 Epstein was
incarcerated for just over one month before he was found dead in his cell on August 10, 2019. 617

        The MCC, and the BOP generally, exhibited recurring and widespread institutional
failures. On June 27, 2023, the DOJ OIG released a report entitled, Investigation and Review of
the Federal Bureau of Prisons’ Custody, Care, and Supervision of Jeffrey Epstein at the
Metropolitan Correctional Center in New York, New York (OIG Report). The OIG Report found
“numerous and serious failures by MCC New York staff, including multiple violations of MCC
New York and BOP policies and procedures:” 618

        Our investigation and review of the Federal Bureau of Prisons’ (BOP)
        custody, care, and supervision of Jeffrey Epstein identified numerous and
        serious failures by employees of the Metropolitan Correctional Center
        located in New York, New York [], including falsifying BOP records
        relating to inmate counts and rounds and multiple violations of MCC New
        York and BOP policies and procedures, which compromised Epstein’s
        safety, the safety of other inmates, and the security of the institution.
        Specifically, we found that MCC New York staff failed to undertake
        required measures designated to make sure that, among other things,
        Epstein and other inmates were accounted for and safe, such as conducting
        inmate counts and 30-minute rounds, searching inmate cells, and ensuring
        adequate supervision of the Special Housing Unit (SHU) and the
        functionality of MCC New York’s security system. 619

        Additional evidence obtained by the Committee demonstrates that these failures were not
isolated occurrences.

                                                       106
        A. BOP and MCC Failures Led to Public Uncertainties.
         According to the OIG Report, the MCC—which closed permanently in 2021—was
understaffed and staffed with personnel that exhibited widespread disregard of BOP policies. 620
Specifically, the OIG Report noted that “staffing shortages…compromised the ability of MCC
New York staff to adequately supervise inmates.” 621 As a result, the OIG Report “found that
multiple BOP employees submitted false documents claiming that they had performed the
required counts and rounds,” including on the night Epstein died. 622 In the days and hours before
Epstein’s death, Epstein’s cellmate was transferred out of his cell, directly in conflict with “the
Psychology Department’s directive that Epstein be assigned a cellmate,” 623 critical surveillance
cameras in the SHU were not recording, 624 required rounds and counts were improperly
performed, 625 and the officers assigned to monitor the unit were either sleeping or engaged in
activities unrelated to their duties. 626

         The evidence suggests that BOP ultimately failed to preserve the basic safeguards,
records, and reliable eyewitness accounts necessary to provide the public with a complete and
unquestionable account of what occurred inside the MCC on the night of August 9, 2019. These
institutional failures contributed to the uncertainty and speculation that continue to surround
Epstein’s death.

i.      Cellmate Incidents and Transfers Left Epstein Alone at Time of Death.
        According to the OIG Report, when Epstein arrived at MCC on July 6, 2019, he was
detained in the SHU and remained in the SHU until his death. 627 The SHU was a separate unit
that housed inmates subject to administrative detention or disciplinary segregation. 628 The SHU
“securely separated [inmates] from the general inmate population and kept locked in their cells
for approximately 23 hours a day, to ensure their own safety as well as the safety of other
inmates.” 629 According to witness testimony, Epstein was housed in the SHU because he was
considered a high-profile inmate. 630

       On July 23, 2019, Epstein attempted suicide and was found unresponsive, but semi-
conscious, with an orange fabric noose around his neck. 631 Epstein claimed his cellmate,

ii.     Inadequate Training and Record Keeping was Commonplace at MCC.
        The Committee interviewed Tova Noel, a correctional officer at MCC who was assigned
to work the Special Housing Unit where Epstein was incarcerated. 640 Protocol mandated that
correctional officers assigned to work in the SHU had to complete special training. 641 Noel,
however, was out on injury leave at the time the special training was administered for SHU
officers. 642 When she returned, she was advised by her supervisor to sign the training log
confirming her completion of the training when she had not completed it. 643 Noel did so, and
was sent to work in the SHU regardless of her lack of training. 644

         Noel was assigned to work in the SHU at the time of Epstein’s death and recounted that
while SHU protocols required there be four officers on any given shift, there were usually only
three. 645 Noel explained that understaffing was a significant source of management and
personnel issues at the MCC. 646 According to Noel, MCC mandated overtime hours to address
the understaffing issues: 647

        Tova Noel (May 18, 2026)

        Q.       And how often would you or other correctional officers in the
                 SHU have to work overtime?

        Regardless of overtime hours, Noel testified that there were still not enough correctional
officers on duty to regularly complete required tasks. 649 “Rounds” were mandated to be
conducted by staff to observe all inmates twice an hour at irregular intervals with “no more than
40 minutes in between.” 650 The “counts,” in contrast, were mandated to be conducted institution-
wide during the 4:00 PM, 10:00 PM, 12:00 AM, 3:00 AM, and 5:00 AM hours. 651 Counts were
required to “ensure that all inmates are present and accounted for at the appropriate location
within the facility.” 652 Per MCC protocol, both rounds and counts were required to be conducted
by two officers. 653 Yet this was not the standard practice at the MCC. Not only were rounds and
counts not conducted on time or at the required frequencies, usually only one officer conducted
them. 654 Noel blamed the long, mandated hours and lack of proper staffing when asked why she
and other correctional officers failed to perform their duties:

        Tova Noel (May 18, 2026)

        On the night of Epstein’s death, Noel admitted she did not properly execute the required

shift, an officer with whom Noel had never previously worked—Michael Thomas—had joined
her. 662 According to Noel, Thomas expressed that he was tired when she went to him to conduct
the required counts, and he subsequently slept for “pretty much for the…duration of the shift.” 663
Thomas did not conduct any rounds or counts for the entirety of his 12:00 AM to 8:00 AM
shift. 664

        Noel further testified to the Committee that, during that same early morning shift, she
was surfing the internet on the desktop computer at the SHU station. 665 At approximately 5:45
AM, after failing to conduct her 12:00 AM, 3:00 AM, and 5:00 AM institutionally mandated
counts, Noel was instead browsing the web. 666 According to a forensic report completed of the
desktop computer, Noel was using a search engine to look up various things, including insurance,
furniture, and—most interestingly—Jeffrey Epstein. 667 Noel’s desktop computer demonstrated
that she twice clicked on “latest on Epstein in jail,” once at 5:42 AM and again at 5:52 AM. 668
Noel maintained she did not directly type in a search for information related to Epstein, but
clicked on links on “the homepage where the news loads:” 669

iii.    The Question of the Blurry Orange Figure
       The video footage of a blurry orange figure moving up the stairs in the general direction
of Epstein’s cell remains a matter of public speculation. 672 According to footage released by the
DOJ, one functioning video camera showed, at 10:39 PM on the night of August 9, 2019, what
appears to be an individual carrying orange linens up the stairs. 673

        Both the DOJ OIG and the FBI analyzed the video clip and described the video as a flash
of orange moving up the L tier stairs. 674 The FBI suggested it might have been a person escorting
an inmate up the stairs, but the OIG noted that inmates were on lockdown for the night and
instead proposed it could be “someone [] carrying inmate linen or bedding up the stairs.” 675

        Noel was unable to provide clarity on the blurred orange figure, but noted that she did not
let anyone in the SHU area at that time:

        A.       No. I was off the camera, that way.

V. President Trump and the 119th Congress Championed
Transparency to Inform the American People.

        On November 19, 2025, President Trump signed the Epstein Files Transparency Act
(EFTA) into law. 690 The EFTA resulted in the public release by DOJ of millions of documents
related to Epstein and Maxwell, shedding light on their criminal schemes and networks which
spanned decades and multiple presidential administrations.

       The Committee also sought to provide the American public with full and complete
transparency during its own investigation, while protecting the identities of survivors. The
Committee’s investigation and commitment to public updates and release of information
substantially expanded the information available to the American people concerning Epstein,
Maxwell, their associates, and the federal government’s handling of their crimes.

        Independently of the Trump Administration’s efforts, the Committee issued 14
subpoenas, conducted 7 depositions and 12 transcribed interviews, passed three related contempt
reports out of the Committee, passed a contempt resolution by unanimous consent on the House
floor, and reviewed hundreds of thousands of documents from JPMorgan Chase, Deutsche Bank,
the Estate, and the U.S. Virgin Islands.

        Prior to the release of files under the EFTA, Chairman Comer issued a subpoena to then-
Attorney General Pamela J. Bondi for the “full, complete, and unredacted Epstein Files” 691 On
August 19, 2025, the DOJ expressly stated that materials would be produced on a rolling basis
starting on August 22, 2025, and that all redactions required by law, regulation, or court order
would be made. 692

       On August 22, 2025, the DOJ produced over 33,000 responsive materials to the
Committee. 693 This included FBI and DOJ case files, court documents, flight logs, email
correspondence, and prison records, among other things. 694 However, DOJ indicated that
“multiple responsive records must be withheld or redacted as required by applicable law”
including certain grand jury materials and documents containing personally identifiable
information. 695 Nonetheless, DOJ expressed a willingness to work collaboratively by

        A. The DOJ Failed to Meet Statutory Requirements Despite Efforts
           to Comply.
     The Trump Administration has repeatedly committed to releasing Epstein-related files.
However, multiple shortcomings related to releasing documents and failures to adequately
communicate about those releases plagued the DOJ’s efforts.

        On February 21, 2025, then-Attorney General Pamela J. Bondi was asked in an interview
about a purported Epstein client list, to which she responded, “It’s sitting on my desk right
now.” 702 One week later, Attorney General Bondi invited a group of independent journalists and
social media influencers to the White House to receive white binders labeled “The Epstein Files:
Phase 1” that contained approximately 300 pages of flight logs and other related Epstein
documents. 703 That same day, Attorney General Bondi sent a letter to FBI Director Kash Patel
requesting all remaining Epstein files be produced to the DOJ. 704 According to the letter, Bondi
had concerns that the FBI had not shared all responsive documents with the DOJ. 705

       However, according to its July 2025 memorandum, the DOJ and FBI concluded, “[after]
an exhaustive review of investigative holdings relating to Jeffrey Epstein,” that “no further
disclosure would be appropriate or warranted.” 706 Notably, the memorandum stated that there
was no “client list:”

        This systematic review revealed no incriminating “client list.” There was
        also no credible evidence found that Epstein blackmailed prominent
        individuals as a part of his actions. We did not uncover evidence that could
        predicate an investigation against uncharged third parties. 707

       In a transcribed interview, Attorney General Bondi clarified that the “client list” that she
said was on her desk was referring to an assortment of uncategorizable files:

         Attorney General Pam Bondi (May 29, 2026)

        Q.       Attorney General Bondi, on February 21st of 2025, just over two
                 weeks after you were sworn in as AG, you responded to a
                 question in an interview about a purported list of Epstein’s
                 clients by saying, “It’s sitting on my desk right now.” Is that an
                 accurate characterization of your statements during that
                 interview?

        On November 18, 2025, the U.S. House of Representatives passed the EFTA. 714
Following passage by the Senate, the EFTA was signed in law by President Trump. 715 The EFTA
required the Attorney General to “make publicly available in a searchable and downloadable
format all unclassified records, documents, communications, and investigative materials in the
possession of the Department of Justice” relating to Jeffrey Epstein (including all investigations,
prosecutions, or custodial matters, Ghislaine Maxwell, flight logs and travel records, named
individuals (including government officials), related entities, immunity deals or settlements,
internal DOJ charging decisions, document handling, and Epstein’s detention or death. 716
Furthermore, the EFTA explicitly stated that no record may be “withheld, delayed, or redacted on

        The first release occurred within a week of the December 19, 2025, deadline. 727 On
January 30, 2026, the DOJ published its fifth EFTA release, which consisted of approximately
3.5 million files. 728 That same day, the DOJ transmitted a letter to Congress that indicated that it
was in “compliance with its production obligations under the [EFTA]” and that unredacted
versions of released materials would be made available for inspection at the DOJ by members of
Congress. 729

       In a press conference announcing the release of documents on January 30, 2026, then
Deputy Attorney General Blanche stated that the DOJ collection effort initially resulted in 6
million pages being identified as potentially responsive:

        A rigorous process has been undertaken to protect victims against any
        clearly unwarranted violation of their personal privacy. This resulted in 6
        million pages being identified as potentially responsive including
        Department and FBI emails, summaries, videos and other previous
        materials collected and generated during the various investigations and
        prosecutions. We aired on the side of over collection …which means that
        the number of responsive [] pages is smaller than the total number initially

        million pages today, and not the 6 million pages that we collected. I want to

         B. The DOJ Failed to Adequately Shield Sensitive Information
            Pertaining to Survivors.
        The DOJ—presumably due to the speed with which files were being reviewed—failed to
adequately redact names and personally identifiable information of numerous survivors. 743
During its investigation, the Committee requested that DOJ address errors and investigate the
sources of those errors in the documents released on multiple occasions. Attorney General Bondi
told the Committee that many of the mistakes in the redactions process was due to “the volume
of documents,” 744 the “intense, short timeframe,” 745 and “hundreds of lawyers [] working around
the clock even on holidays to get these documents out.” 746 Bondi also stated that Blanche
informed her the error rate was around one percent, but even if one name was inadvertently
released then “that[] [was] a failure.” 747

VI. Findings
   At a minimum, Groff chose to turn a blind eye to the criminal conduct that was occurring
    as she facilitated and arranged massage appointments for Epstein. The DOJ should
    further question and otherwise investigate Groff’s likely knowledge of and facilitation of
    Epstein’s criminal activity.

   It is not credible that, after years of serving as Epstein’s executive assistant and regularly
    arranging massages for him, Groff could not recall the names or accompanying
    descriptors in Epstein’s Black Book, or that she never questioned who these individuals
    were or how they had been identified as potential “masseuses.”

   Maxwell was the original creator of Epstein’s directory, the Black Book, while other
    assistants—including Groff—had the ability to access and edit the information within
    without ever questioning its details.

   Epstein recruited women into his orbit by posing, falsely, as a Victoria’s Secret model
    scout. By holding himself out as a scout, Epstein was able to meet with and, from there,
    take advantage of numerous young aspiring models. Wexner, owner of L Brands
    company, did not authorize Epstein to hold himself out as affiliated with Victoria’s
    Secret.

   Epstein and Fekkai worked together to recruit young women and girls, specifically those
    who were aspiring models, to gain access to these women through control and abuse.

   Following his 2008 arrest, Epstein continued to recruit young women and girls from
    vulnerable circumstances, whom he placed in roles as his “personal assistants.” This
    arrangement provided a new conduit through which Epstein and Maxwell could maintain
    access to and control over these individuals, and it was through these “assistant” roles
    that Epstein continued to exploit and abuse young women and girls beyond the earlier
    pretext of massage services.

   Epstein sexually assaulted minors under the guise of massages for years. The individuals
    within Epstein’s close orbit, by turning a blind eye, enabled him to continue his criminal
    activities with these young victims after his 2008 arrest without scrutiny or prosecution.

   One of the defining characteristics of Epstein’s abuse network was the systematic
    creation of financial, emotional, and professional dependency among many of the young
    women and girls he targeted. Through gifts, cash payments, promises of opportunity, and
    manipulation, Epstein established relationships that strengthened his influence and helped
    enable his system of exploitation.

   Epstein used his connections with prominent individuals to bolster his sense of power,
    establish his legitimacy, secure access to information, and insulate himself from any
    repercussions—particularly in the eyes of the young women around him.


                                              130
 Kellen’s recruitment was consistent with a pattern documented across multiple survivors’
  accounts in which Epstein sought out women experiencing financial or personal
  hardships and exploited those vulnerabilities to facilitate ongoing abuse.

 Epstein used code names, specifically Disney princesses, to identify and communicate
  about young women and girls with associates like Staley and Levine.

 Acosta exercised a hands-off approach and took responsibility for his failure to oversee
  the federal prosecution of Epstein that left the investigation in the hands of potential
  Epstein loyalists, specifically Menchel and Reinhart.

 Menchel was potentially connected to Epstein during his time at USAO, which could
  have impacted his decision making in investigating and in entering into an NPA with
  Epstein.

 Reinhart may have been in a position to influence the Epstein investigation or relay
  information about the investigation to Epstein given evidence suggesting their
  preparation to work together while he was still employed at the USAO.

 The NPA was an inappropriate conclusion for the Epstein matter and led federal
  prosecutors to neglect key elements of their investigation in favor of a swift and
  inadequate resolution.

 Epstein’s 2008 work release amounted to an irresponsible abuse of discretion by the
  PBSO and was rife with financial conflicts.

 The investigation was inadequate as law enforcement never fully explored allegations in
  other states like New York and New Mexico before concluding their investigation with
  the NPA.

 The USAO failed in their duties to uphold the NPA by not seeking a claim of breach by
  Epstein over his excessive work release.

 Federal prosecutors failed to keep survivors informed about the existence of an NPA,
  delaying justice by hampering their ability to bring civil claims or to otherwise publicly
  expose Epstein’s crimes.

 NDAs were used to silence survivors and obfuscate criminal activity.

 Epstein deliberately cultivated relationships with wealthy and prominent people because
  those relationships carried value beyond any individual transaction. He repeatedly used
  an association with one respected person to establish credibility with another.

 Epstein made himself difficult to abandon by providing business referrals, investments,
  employment, education, loans, charitable support, introductions, travel, and personal
  assistance to people around him.

                                           131
 Epstein’s 2008 conviction did not sever him from elite financial and professional circles.
  Although some associates ended their relationships, other prominent individuals
  continued and expanded dealings with him after he became a registered sex offender.

 Uninterrupted association with prominent people helped rehabilitate Epstein’s standing.
  Witnesses testified that the presence of respected business contacts caused them to
  discount warning signs and made Epstein’s representations about his legitimacy appear
  more credible.

 Epstein’s relationships secured institutional access. Banks processed his transactions,
  professional advisers administered his entities and finances, travel providers arranged
  domestic and international movement, and academic relationships gave him continuing
  access to prominent researchers and other influential people.

 Financial institutions possessed substantial information regarding Epstein’s criminal
  history and repeatedly identified activity presenting heightened compliance risks. Yet
  those risks were not adequately addressed given Epstein’s inside connections.

 The U.S. and foreign governments failed to identify clear indicators of human trafficking
  and immigration fraud. This fraud and trafficking were enabled by institutions like
  American Express, whose own agents allowed Epstein to use fraudulent decoy flights and
  refundable or dummy itineraries to move foreign women across borders directly into
  Epstein’s residences.

 As a result of understaffing and systemic dysfunction at MCC, the correctional officers
  on both shifts on the night of Epstein’s death missed—at a minimum—12 rounds and 3
  counts, leading to an unacceptable lack of supervision of Epstein and all SHU inmates
  that totaled nearly 8 hours.

 Evidence received by the Committee did not disprove the finding of the joint
  investigation between the Southern District of New York and the FBI which concluded
  that Epstein’s death occurred in a manner reflecting suicide.

 The Trump Administration and the Committee’s investigation expanded public access to
  records concerning Jeffrey Epstein and Ghislaine Maxwell, advancing transparency into
  their criminal activities and the individuals and institutions associated with them.

 The Department of Justice failed to adequately communicate regarding releases of
  Epstein related documents, and files released pursuant to the Epstein Files Transparency
  Act failed to adequately protect survivors, including through deficient redactions that
  exposed their sensitive information.

 Oversight Democrats concentrated their investigative efforts on President Trump rather
  than the broader, decades long institutional failures that enabled Epstein’s abuse and
  robbed survivors of swift justice.


                                          132
VII. Conclusion and Recommendations
       Epstein and Maxwell orchestrated an elaborate, long-term recruitment network in which
they groomed young women and girls, forced and lured them into their orbit, and subjected them
to abuse and exploitation. Epstein and Maxwell were able to evade scrutiny for years due to a
wide array of governmental, institutional, and individual failures. Epstein and Maxwell
maintained a sophisticated network of prominent and affluent associates and loyal employees
who enabled them to continue their criminal activities.

       The purpose of the Committee’s investigation was to identify the failures that allowed
Epstein and Maxwell to avoid scrutiny for their criminal activities for so long and to use those
findings to propose legislation and further avenues of oversight to enhance justice for survivors
and prevent such a situation from recurring.

       Notwithstanding significant limitations given the passage of time, the fact that Epstein’s
crimes spanned decades, and the death of key witnesses, the Committee’s investigation was
thorough. The Committee acquired documents and garnered testimonies from government
agencies, judicial systems, financial institutions, personal estates, and from some of the world’s
most influential and prominent individuals including multiple billionaires, former and current
Cabinet members, and a former President. Throughout its investigation, the Committee sought to
uncover the truth about the crimes of Epstein and Maxwell and provide as much justice and
accountability as possible for the survivors of these heinous crimes.

        The crimes committed by Epstein after 2005 could have been prevented and justice
served had it not been for the repeated failures of law enforcement and federal prosecutors. In the
Committee’s investigation into the circumstances surrounding the federal investigation, failures
mounted over years: the non-prosecution agreement, the absurdly lenient treatment of Epstein
during his state sentence, and the failure to detect clear signs of human trafficking empowered
Epstein and delayed or deprived survivors of justice. Many factors played into Epstein’s evasion
of justice—including poor judgment on the part of the U.S. Attorneys Office—but likely the
most significant factor was that Epstein shielded himself by using his pervasive and wide-
reaching influence. These failures contributed to an outcome that did not adequately account for
the interests and needs of the survivors.

       Epstein was arrested on federal sex trafficking charges in 2019, but he died while in
custody, in his cell at the MCC in New York, on August 10, 2019. Grave institutional failures and
widespread dysfunction at the MCC led to legitimate questions surrounding Epstein’s death.

         Maxwell was subsequently arrested in 2020 for the exploitation and trafficking of minors
in relation to the crimes she conspired to commit with Epstein. Maxwell was convicted and is
currently serving a 20-year sentence at Federal Prison Camp Bryan in Bryan, Texas. Maxwell
asserted her fifth amendment rights against answering the Committee’s questions during her
deposition.

        Epstein and Maxwell recruited a vast number of young women and girls and sexually
assaulted them over years. Following his initial arrest, Epstein and Maxwell continued to recruit

                                               133
young women and girls from vulnerable circumstances, whom they placed in the role of
“personal assistant.” This arrangement provided a new conduit through which Epstein and
Maxwell could maintain access to and control over these individuals; it was through these
“assistant” roles that Epstein continued to exploit and abuse young women and girls beyond the
earlier pretext of massage services.

        Epstein and Maxwell intentionally cultivated financial and professional dependency
among these young women and girls by providing tuition and housing assistance, facilitating
visas, providing employment, and leveraging their connections with influential figures. Many of
these women were subjected to repeated abuse by Epstein, Maxwell, and their associates. To
avoid scrutiny for their actions, Epstein and Maxwell retained a web of loyal employees,
purchasing their allegiance through cash payments and expensive gifts. In turn, these
employees—specifically Richard Kahn, Darren Indyke, and Lesley Groff—turned a blind eye
and enabled Epstein’s criminal activities to continue.

        Epstein and Maxwell also maintained a rolodex of influential, wealthy, and famous
individuals. Epstein used these connections as currency, frequently inflating or fraudulently
representing one connection to gain another. Each link in this network enhanced Epstein’s
perceived status and influence, allowing him to present himself as far more powerful and well-
connected than he otherwise might have appeared. Epstein’s powerful associations allowed him
to avoid scrutiny for his criminal acts for many years.

                                           Recommendations

       Congress should pass legislation such as Chairman Comer’s H.R. 10389, the
        Survivor’s Voice Protection Act, to ensure witnesses and survivors of sexual abuse are
        not deterred from speaking freely by abusive non-disclosure or confidentiality
        agreements.

        The Committee’s investigation revealed how powerful individuals use and weaponize
non-disclosure and other confidentiality agreements to silence survivors of sexual abuse and
trafficking. The Committee expresses appreciation to the survivors and their representatives for
their willingness to engage in continual conversation to inform this investigation. The Committee
notes that its receipt of information from survivors and their representatives was often on
condition of maintaining anonymity. Although the Committee routinely handles confidential
sources to advance its oversight objectives, it is notable that many survivors, despite Epstein’s
demise, remain fearful of retaliation and lawfare by Epstein or his powerful associates if those
survivors’ identities or knowledge were to become matters of public record. The Committee
understands that misuse and abuse of nondisclosure and confidentiality agreements by Epstein or
associates enabled his crimes and may even still be shielding him and others from accountability.

       This is why Chairman Comer introduced legislation, H.R. 10389, the Survivor’s Voice
Protection Act, 770 to ensure that individuals with information about sexual abuse and survivors
can speak freely and openly, despite any non-disclosure or confidentiality agreement. The bill

770
   Press Release, H. Comm. on Oversight & Gov’t Reform, Chairman Comer Introduces the Survivor’s Voice
Protection Act (Sept. 16, 2026).

                                                    134
also takes steps to prevent the grooming of minors and ensure accountability for those seeking to
exploit children. The legislation applies retroactively, covering agreements predating the
legislation and defines grooming behavior for the first time in federal law. This bill’s reforms are
a direct result of evidence received during the Committee’s investigation.

       DOJ should provide an appropriate process for Epstein survivors and their
        representatives to access their personal files and records in unredacted form.

        The Committee understands that many survivors have largely been unable to review their
own records held by DOJ. Many survivors have also expressed concerns that the DOJ’s handling
and organization of the EFTA release has hindered their ability to locate and obtain potentially
significant information that could support their legal cases. 771 The DOJ should provide Epstein
survivors with access to their personal files and records in unredacted form to empower them to
seek justice.

       DOJ should ensure that investigators interview or reinterview key witnesses to
        ensure justice for survivors of Epstein’s and Maxwell’s crimes.

         One thing is abundantly clear in the Epstein matter: the federal government failed over
the course of decades and multiple administrations to ensure justice and protect Epstein
survivors. DOJ must make renewed efforts to provide this needed justice and accountability to
Epstein survivors. Too many survivors and other witnesses informed this Committee that they
were never approached or properly questioned by federal authorities. The DOJ should interview
or, in some cases, reinterview key individuals who were part of the inner circle of Epstein and
Maxwell.

       Non-Prosecution Agreements (NPAs) must be scrutinized closely to ensure they
        serve the purposes of justice, and do not instead enable and empower criminals.

       The Southern District of Florida’s NPA with Epstein was wholly inappropriate. The
agreement shielded Epstein and Maxwell from accountability and enabled Epstein to continue
committing crimes until his arrest in 2019. DOJ must reform its use of NPAs. These reforms
should protect survivors’ rights and interests in receiving justice, ensure that NPAs are used only
in appropriate circumstances, and require review by senior DOJ officials.

       DOJ and the Offices of the United States Attorneys must ensure rigorous
        enforcement of policies designed to prevent conflicts of interest from materializing
        in investigations and prosecutions.

        DOJ should consider enhancing policies and disclosure requirements intended to prevent
conflicts of interest, including by providing additional training to AUSAs. Epstein was found to
have had ties to lawyers from the office responsible for prosecuting him. The American public––
along with survivors of sexual abuse and trafficking––must remain confident that lawyers


  James Hill, et al., Epstein’s alleged victims accuse DOJ of legal violations over state of files released, ABC
771

NEWS (Dec. 22, 2025).

                                                         135
representing DOJ in criminal matters do not have conflicts of interest that call into question their
zealous advocacy on behalf of DOJ.

    The U.S. Department of Treasury (Treasury) and FinCEN should review whether
     financial institutions are meeting their obligations under reporting requirements
     designed to prevent suspicious activities such as money laundering and human
     trafficking.

        Treasury should ensure financial institutions are meeting their existing reporting
requirements, and review whether updates to regulations implementing provisions of the Bank
Secrecy Act or other statutes are necessary to ensure financial institutions do not shield
prominent clients from scrutiny. The Committee uncovered information during the investigation
that banks turned a blind eye to clearly suspicious activities of Epstein and his close associates.

        The Committee understands that many required filings by banks in the Epstein matter
that should have been raised when they detected suspicious activity were instead made
retroactively after Epstein’s arrest in 2019. If financial institutions had filed the requisite
information in a timely manner, it is likely that many of the crimes committed by Epstein and
Maxwell would have been detected earlier. Financial institutions must be held accountable to
reporting requirements for suspicious repeated payments, rapid movement of funds through
various accounts, and financial activity inconsistent with stated business purposes regardless of
the prominence of a client or their value to the institution.

                                                ***

        For far too long, Jeffrey Epstein operated with impunity, shielded and enabled by
powerful connections and a justice system that failed the survivors of his crimes for decades. The
United States should not be a haven for human or sex trafficking. Governments at all levels and
powerful institutions must comply with legal obligations designed to detect these crimes, impede
the perpetrators, and bring justice for survivors.
